Symplectic cacti, virtualization and Berenstein–Kirillov groups
Olga Azenhas, Mojdeh Tarighat Feller, Jacinta Torres

Abstract
. We explicitly realize an internal action of the symplectic cactus group, recently defined by Halacheva for any complex, reductive, finite-dimensional Lie algebra, on crystals of Kashiwara–Nakashima tableaux. Our methods include a symplectic version of jeu de taquin due to Sheats and Lecouvey, symplectic reversal, and virtualization due to Baker. As an application, we define and study a symplectic version of the Berenstein–Kirillov group and show that it is a quotient of the symplectic cactus group. In addition two relations for symplectic Berenstein–Kirillov group are given that do not follow from the defining relations of the symplectic cactus group.



2000 Mathematics Subject Classification
05E10, 05E05, 17B37

1.
Introduction

The
cactus group
was originally defined by Henriques–Kamnitzer

[
hk06
]
in the context of coboundary categories defined by
Drinfeld
[
Drinfeld1990
]
. Coboundary categories are monoidal
categories equipped with a
commutor
, that is, a collection of
natural isomorphisms
\sigma_{A,B}:A\otimes B\rightarrow B\otimes A

satisfying certain properties. The idea of studying the cactus group was
originally due to A. Berenstein and was taken up by Henriques–Kamnitzer
in
[
hk06
]
, who defined it and further showed that it can be
realized as the fundamental group of the moduli space of marked real
genus zero stable curves. The original idea of Berenstein was to
construct a commutor in the category of crystals of a complex, reductive,
finite-dimensional Lie algebra, by first defining an involution

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xib}{{{}}\xi_{\textsf{B}}}:\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{B}{{{}}\textsf{B}}\rightarrow\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{B}{{{}}\textsf{B}}
for each crystal

B
which flips the crystal by exchanging highest weight
elements with lowest weight elements. In the case of

\mathfrak{sl}(n,C)
with the tableau model for the highest weight
crystal
B
(λ)
it was known that

\xi_{\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{cncrystal}{{{}}\textsf{B}(\lambda)}}
coincides with the Schützenberger
involution on semi-standard Young tableaux of shape
\lambda

[
bz96
]
. See
[
BSch17
, Sections 4.3, 14.3.3]
and the
references therein.

Let
g
be a complex, semisimple Lie
algebra with Dynkin diagram
X
. There is a Dynkin diagram automorphism

\theta:X\rightarrow X
defined by
\alpha_{\theta(i)}=-w_{0}\alpha_{i}
, where
w_{0}
is the longest element of the Weyl
group of
g
. The
cactus group
J_{\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}}}
, defined by Halacheva in
[
ha20
,
ha16
]
, is
the group generated by
\sigma_{I}
, where
I
runs over all connected
sub-Dynkin diagrams of
X
, subject to the following relations:

\displaystyle\sigma_{I}^{2}

\displaystyle=1,

(1)

\displaystyle\sigma_{I}\sigma_{J}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{J}\sigma_{I}\hbox{ if }J\subseteq X,\,J\cup I\hbox{ is disconnected }

(2)

\displaystyle\sigma_{I}\sigma_{J}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\theta_{I}(J)}\sigma_{I}\hbox{ if }J\subset I

(3)

where
\theta_{I}
is the automorphism on
I
defined by the longest
element of the parabolic group
W^{I}
. Halacheva has defined an
internal action of the cactus group
J_{\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}}}
on a normal

g
–crystal by partial Schützenberger–Lusztig
involutions
\xi_{I}
. From this action we know that partial
Schützenberger–Lusztig involutions satisfy the cactus group
J_{\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}}}
relations
[
hakarywe20
]
. Halacheva
[
ha20
]

initiated a combinatorial study of the cactus group for
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C})
by comparing the action of
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Jn}{{{}}J_{n}}=J_{\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C})}
on a normal
\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C})
-crystal with that of the
Berenstein–Kirillov group on Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns (or semi-standard
Young tableaux)
[
bk95
]
. Using a different approach, Chmutov, Glick
and Pylyavskyy
[
cgp16
]
have also found relationships between those
two groups.

Our results compose a combinatorial study of the cactus group for the
symplectic Lie algebra
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})
.
There are many combinatorial models for
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})
-crystals: De Concini tableaux

[
deconcini
]
, King tableaux
[
King75
]
, Lakshmibai-Seshadri

[
lakseshadri
]
and Littelmann paths
[
lit95
,
lit97
]
, the alcove path model of Gaussent–Littelmann
[
GL05
]

and the one of Lenart–Postnikov
[
lenpos08
]
, but we work
with Kashiwara–Nakashima tableaux, for which a rich combinatorial
structure exists
[
kn91
,
honkang
,
lec02
,
lec07
]
. We review the basics in Sections
3
and
4
. For each connected sub-Dynkin diagram
I
of
X
, we define the explicit action of
\xi_{I}
on a given Kashiwara–Nakashima tableau. The algorithmic procedure for that action is given by virtualization. In the case when
I
forms a Dynkin diagram of type
C_{n-k}
, it is also given by the
I
-partial symplectic reversal
, a symplectic analogue of partial reversal on
A_{n-1}
tableaux. Thereby we provide a combinatorial action of the cactus generators
\sigma_{I}
on the set of Kashiwara–Nakashima tableaux on the alphabet
C

n

. This is addressed in Sections
8
and
9
. The case of
I=X
has already been developed by Santos in
[
sa21a
]
, where he defines an operation
on straight shaped Kashiwara–Nakashima tableaux which is a

symplectic analogue
of the Schützenberger involution
operation, also known as evacuation, on straight shaped
A_{n-1}

semi-standard Young tableaux. This procedure includes the symplectic
jeu-de-taquin defined by Sheats in
[
she99
]
, and further developed
by Lecouvey
[
lec02
]
using crystal isomorphisms. This is the content
of Section
7
.

For
\ I\subseteq X
such that
I
forms a Dynkin diagram of type

C_{n-k}
, we define an algorithm for

I
-partial symplectic
reversal
which generalizes Santos’ algorithm in the sense that, when
I=X
, our algorithm is exactly the same. The symplectic
C_{n-k}

reversal extends symplectic
C_{n-k}
evacuation to arbitrary
semi-standard skew tableaux on the alphabet
\mathcal{C}_{n-k}
whose
shift of the entries by
k
are admissible on the alphabet

C

n

. The
C_{n-k}
reversal of a such
semi-standard skew tableau
P
on the alphabet
\mathcal{C}_{n-k}
,
is characterized to be the unique skew tableau coplactic equivalent to

P
and plactic equivalent to the
C_{n-k}
evacuation of the
symplectic rectification of
P
.

An important inspiration behind our generalization is the operation of

tableau-switching

[
bss
]
of Benkart, Sottile and Stroomer
on
A_{n-1}
semi-standard tableaux. Given an admissible tableau on the
alphabet
C

n

, we start off by freezing the entries
corresponding to nodes not appearing in
I
, creating at the same time a
new Young tableau
U
with Young shape defined by the positive frozen
entries as well as a skew tableau
P
consisting of the non-frozen
entries. The tableau pair
(U,P)
, sharing a common border, pass
through each other via
symplectic jeu de taquin
(SJDT for short).
After performing this procedure, a new pair
(R,V)
with
R
the
symplectic rectification of
P
and
V
consisting of the entries of
U

as well as some new,
colored
letters. Each color records a
precise instance of the symplectic rectification of
P
. Our

symplectic colourful tableau switching
is reversible since SJDT
is reversible. It reduces to the
A_{n-1}
tableau switching on
tableaux in the alphabet
[n]
. This work is carried out in detail in
Subsection
9.2
of this paper, yielding the formula (
52
), and illustrated in
Subsection
9.3
.

For the general case we use the virtualization map defined by Baker

[
ba00a
]
, that is, an injective map

\displaystyle E:\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{symptab}{{{}}\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)}\longrightarrow\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{ssytv}{{{}}\textsf{SSYT}(\lambda^{A},n,\bar{n})}

which assigns to the
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})
-crystal
KN
(λ,n)
, a
subset of the
\mathfrak{sl}(2n,\mathbb{C})
-crystal
\textsf{SSYT}(\lambda^{A},n,\,\bar{n})
in a reversible way. This is discussed in Section

5
. We show that one may apply the map
E
, then
perform a certain partial Schützenberger–Lusztig involution in the
type
\mathfrak{sl}(2n,\mathbb{C})
-crystal without leaving the image of
E
, reverse
the virtualization map
E
and obtain our desired result. Subsection
9.4.1
provides Theorem

5
and Theorem
6
with such algorithmic procedures.
Additionally, in Definition
3
, Section
6
, we
define the
virtual symplectic cactus group

~

J

2
n

and
show that it is a subgroup of
J_{2n}
isomorphic to the symplectic
cactus group
J

s
p
(
2
n
,
C
)

. In Theorem
3
, Section

8
, an action of the virtual
symplectic cactus group on the set
SSYT
(λ

A

,n,
¯

n

)
is defined. The subset

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{E}{{{}}E}(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{symptab}{{{}}\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)})
is preserved under this action as shown in Subsections

9.4.1
and
9.5
. In
particular, in Subsection
9.5
, we realize such action
of the virtual symplectic cactus group on the virtual images of
Kashiwara–Nakashima tableaux and show that it
virtualizes
the
action of the symplectic cactus group on Kashiwara–Nakashima tableaux.
This work is illustrated in Section
.

As an application, in Section
, we define

symplectic
Bender–Knuth involutions combinatorially
(Definition
). We start off by defining the type
C_{n}

Berenstein–Kirillov group
BK

C

n

as the free group
generated by the partial symplectic Schützenberger–Lusztig
involutions with respect to connected subdiagrams of the type
C_{n}

Dynkin diagram of the form
I=[n]
modulo the relations they satisfy on
Kashiwara–Nakashima tableaux of any straight shape in the alphabet

C

n

. These generators of
BK

C

n

satisfy the
relations of the symplectic cactus group (Theorem
). We show that symplectic
Bender–Knuth involutions are also generators of
BK

C

n

.

We study relations for
BK

C

n

under the virtualization map

E
. More precisely, we consider the relations satisfied by the
embedding of generators of
BK

C

n

in

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{E}{{{}}E}(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{symptab}{{{}}\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)})

\subseteq\textsf{SSYT}(\lambda^{A},n,\,\bar{n})
; we
call this group (Definition
) the
virtual
symplectic Berenstein–Kirillov group

~

B
K

2
n

, a subgroup of the type
A_{2n-1}
Berenstein–Kirillov group

\mathcal{BK}_{2n}
satisfying, in particular, the relations of the
virtual cactus group

~

J

2
n

(Theorem
). Proposition

gives the virtual symplectic
Bender–Knuth involutions generators of

~

B
K

2
n

which are shown in
Theorem
to be the virtualization of
the symplectic Bender–Knuth involutions. The virtual image of the
group
BK

C

n

satisfies the relations of

~

B
K

2
n

. Some of the ones listed in Proposition
are
obtained by applying the partial inverse to the virtualization map. Relations (
) and
(
) in Proposition
are the only ones that do not follow from the the symplectic
cactus group
J

s
p
(
2
n
,
C
)

. They are instead equivalent to the braid relations of type
C_{n}
Weyl group. In particular, relation
(
) is not similar to any relation in previous Berenstein–Kirillov groups.

]
.

3.
Basics

Let
g
be a finite dimensional, complex, semisimple Lie algebra. Let
I
be the Dynkin diagram associated to the root system of
g
,
\Delta=\{\alpha_{i}:i\in I\}
the set of simple roots,
W
its Weyl group, generated by the simple reflections
\{r_{i}:i\in I\}
, and
w_{0}\in W
the longest Weyl group element. We will use the numbering of the vertices of
I
given by
[
bourbakirootsystems
]
. The Dynkin diagram has an automorphism, a permutation of its nodes which leaves the diagram invariant,
\theta:I\rightarrow I
defined by
\alpha_{\theta(i)}=-w_{0}\alpha_{i}
, for any node
i\in I
, where
w_{0}
is the longest element of
W
. We will also denote by
\Lambda
the integral weight lattice associated to the root system of
g
. It is generated by the fundamental weights
\omega_{i}
,
i\in I
. For a connected sub-diagram of
I
,
J\subseteq I
, denote by
\theta_{J}:J\rightarrow J
the Dynkin diagram automorphism that satisfies
\alpha_{\theta_{J}(j)}=-w_{0}^{J}\alpha_{j}
, for any node
j\in J
, where
w_{0}^{J}
is the longest element of the parabolic subgroup
W^{J}\subseteq W
(the Weyl group for
g
restricted to
J
)
[
BB05
]
. When
J=I
one has the original notation
\theta_{I}=\theta
. We focus on the cases where
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C}),\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})
. We will often abuse notation and write a Dynkin diagram
I
with
n
nodes as the interval
[n]
. The corresponding Weyl groups are the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
on
n
letters and the hyperoctahedral group
B_{n}
respectively, where
B_{n}
is the free group generated by
r_{1},\dots,r_{n-1},r_{n}
subject to the relations

\displaystyle r_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=1,\,1\leq i\leq n,

(4)

\displaystyle(r_{i}r_{j})^{2}

\displaystyle=1,\,1\leq i<j\leq n,\,|i-j|>1,

(5)

\displaystyle(r_{i}r_{i+1})^{3}

\displaystyle=1,1\leq i\leq n-2,

(6)

\displaystyle(r_{n-1}r_{n})^{4}

\displaystyle=1.

(7)

The free group generated by
r_{1},\dots,r_{n-1}
, subject to the relations above, for
1\leq i,j<n
, is
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
realized by the simple transpositions
r_{i}=(i,i+1)
on the set
[n]
. The group
B_{n}
has
2^{n}n!
elements and is realized by the signed transpositions
r_{i}=(i,i+1)(\overline{i},\overline{i+1})
,
i=1,\ldots,n-1
, and
r_{n}=(n,\overline{n})
on the set
\{1<\cdots<n<\bar{n}<\cdots<\bar{2}<\bar{1}\}
. That is, we may see
B_{n}
embedded in
\mathfrak{S}_{2n}
by folding
\{1<\cdots<n<\bar{n}<\cdots<\bar{2}<\bar{1}\}
through a central symmetry. The long element of
B_{n}
has length
n^{2}
, while the long element of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
has length
n(n-1)/2
. For instance,
r_{1}r_{2}r_{1}r_{2}=r_{2}r_{1}r_{2}r_{1}
is the long element of
B_{2}
, and, more generally,
(r_{n}\cdots r_{2}r_{1})^{n}=(r_{1}r_{2}\cdots r_{n})^{n}
is the long element of
B_{n}

[
BB05
]
.

Occasionally, for the sake of clarity, we write
w_{0}^{A}
and
w_{0}^{C}
for the corresponding longest elements of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and
B_{n}
respectively, or simply
w_{0}
when there is no room for confusion. Given a vector
v\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, we have that
r_{i}
, with
i\in[n-1]
, acts on
v
,
r_{i}v
, swapping the
i
-th and the
(i+1)
-th entries, and
r_{n}
acts on
v
,
r_{n}v
, changing the sign of the last entry. Henceforth,
w_{0}^{A}
reverses
v
,
w_{0}^{A}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})=(v_{n},\dots,v_{1})
, and
w_{0}^{C}
changes the sign of the entries of
v
,
w_{0}^{C}v=-v
.

Recall the
\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C})
simple roots
\alpha_{i}=\textbf{e}_{i}-\textbf{e}_{i+1}
,
i\in[n-1]
, and the
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})
simple roots
\alpha_{i}=\textbf{e}_{i}-\textbf{e}_{i+1}
,
i\in[n-1]
and
\alpha_{n}=2\textbf{e}_{n}
, where
\textbf{e}_{i}
,
i\in[n]
, is the
\mathbb{R}^{n}
standard basis. The
A_{n-1}
Dynkin diagram automorphisms above, since
-w_{0}\alpha_{i}=-(-\alpha_{n-i})=\alpha_{n-i}
, is given by

\theta(i)=n-i
, with
i\in I=[n-1]
.
For instance,

n=5\qquad A_{4}\qquad\hbox to114.1pt{\vbox to32.79pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.83301pt\lower-13.38383pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

The
C_{n}
Dynkin diagram automorphisms above, since for
w_{0}\in B_{n}
,
-w_{0}\alpha_{i}=-(-\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{i}
, is given by
\theta(i)=i
, with
i\in I=[n]
. The weight lattices are
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}^{n}
for
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})
and
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}^{n}/(1,...,1)
for
\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C})
. We will often work with representatives in the case of
\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C})
. The fundamental weights are
\omega_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{i}e_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
and respectively have representatives
\omega_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n-1
.

3.1.
Levi sub-diagrams

Let
I
be a finite Dynkin diagram. A Levi sub-diagram
J
of
I
obtained by deleting from
I
a subset of its nodes is the Dynkin diagram of a semi-simple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{J}\subset\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}}
known as a
Levi sub-algebra
which is the Levi component of the parabolic Lie sub-algebra of
g
generated by the Chevalley generators associated to the nodes of
J
.

Example 1
.

If we remove the last node (the one labelled by
n
) from the Dynkin diagram of type
C_{n}
, we obtain a Dynkin diagram of type
A_{n-1}
.
which corresponds to the Levi sub-algebra
\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C})
of
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})
.

Example 2
.

The semisimple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}(3,\mathbb{C})\times\mathfrak{sp}(4,\mathbb{C})
is a Levi sub-algebra of
\mathfrak{sp}(12,\mathbb{C})
. Note that the semisimple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C})\times\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})
is not a Levi sub-algebra of
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})
, as its Dynkin diagram of type
A_{n-1}\times A_{1}
cannot be obtained from the type
C_{n}
diagram by deleting some of its vertices.

4.
Normal
\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C})
,
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})
-crystals and Levi restrictions

Crystals corresponding to finite-dimensional (quantum group)
U_{q}(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}})
-representations belong to a family of crystals called
normal crystals

[
BSch17
,
hakarywe20
]
. In classical types, these crystals may be realized by a tableau model
[
kn91
]
and have nice combinatorial properties. Normal crystals arise as the crystals associated to the finite-dimensional representations of a quantum group
U_{q}(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}})
for some Lie algebra
g

[
BSch17
]
. These crystals decompose into connected components, one for each irreducible component to the representation at hand. The Levi restriction of a normal crystal is still a normal crystal, and the union of some connected components of a normal crystal is also a normal crystal
[
BSch17
,
hakarywe20
]
. The crystals that we deal with are tableau crystals for finite-dimensional representations of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C}))
and
U_{q}({\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})})
.

A
g
-crystal is a finite set
B
along with maps

\textsf{wt}:\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{B}{{{}}\textsf{B}}\rightarrow\Lambda,\quad e_{i},f_{i}:\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{B}{{{}}\textsf{B}}\rightarrow\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{B}{{{}}\textsf{B}}\cup\{0\},\,\varepsilon_{i},\varphi_{i}:\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{B}{{{}}\textsf{B}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z},\,

obeying the following axioms for any
b,b^{\prime}\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{B}{{{}}\textsf{B}}
and
i\in I
,

•

b^{\prime}=e_{i}(b)
if and only if
b=f_{i}(b^{\prime})
,

•

if
f_{i}(b)\neq 0
then
\textsf{wt}(f_{i}(b))=\textsf{wt}(b)-\alpha_{i}
;

if
e_{i}(b)\neq 0
, then

\textsf{wt}(e_{i}(b))=\textsf{wt}(b)+\alpha_{i}
, and

•

\varepsilon_{i}(b)=\max\{a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}:e_{i}^{a}(b)\neq 0\}
and

\varphi_{i}(b)=\max\{a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}:f_{i}^{a}(b)\neq 0\}.

•

\varphi_{i}(b)-\varepsilon_{i}(b)=\langle\textsf{wt}(b),\alpha_{i}^{\vee}\rangle
,

where
\alpha_{i}^{\vee}=\frac{2\alpha_{i}}{\langle\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i}\rangle}
are the coroots.

Remark 1
.

Our abstract
g
-crystals are defined with the additional condition that they are seminormal
[
BSch17
]
.

The
crystal graph
of
B
is the directed graph with vertices in
B
and edges labelled by
i\in I
. If
f_{i}(b)=b^{\prime}
for
b,b^{\prime}\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{B}{{{}}\textsf{B}}
, then we draw an edge
b\rightarrow b^{\prime}
. See Example
4
. Given an arbitrary subset
J\subseteq I
,
B

J

is defined to be the crystal
B
restricted to the sub-diagram
J
of
I
, the Levi branched crystal. The crystal graph of
B

J

has the same vertices as
B
, but the arrows are only those labelled in
J
; that is, we forget the maps
e_{i},f_{i},\varphi_{i},
and
\varepsilon_{i}
, for
i\not\in J

[
BSch17
]
. The weight map is
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{B}{{{}}\textsf{B}}\overset{\textsf{wt}}{\rightarrow}\Lambda\overset{can}{\rightarrow}\Lambda_{J}
, where
wt
is the weight map of
B
,
\Lambda
is the weight lattice of
g
,
\Lambda_{J}=\Lambda/<\omega_{i}:i\notin J>
is the weight lattice of
\mathfrak{g}_{J}
, and
\Lambda\overset{can}{\rightarrow}\Lambda_{J}
is the canonical projection. If
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})
and we restrict to
J=[n-1]
, then we obtain a
\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C})
-crystal. For instance, if we restrict an
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})
-crystal to
J=[n-1]
, then we obtain an
\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C})
-crystal. Given
b\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{B}{{{}}\textsf{B}}
,
\textsf{B}(b)
denotes the connected component of
B
containing
b
.

A
g
-crystal is
normal
if it is isomorphic to a disjoint union of the crystals
B
(λ)
, where
B
(λ)
is the crystal associated to an irreducible, finite-dimensional
g
-representation
of highest weight

\lambda
, where
\lambda\in\Lambda
is a
dominant weight
. In this work, where we focus on
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})
, respectively
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C})
, dominant weights in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, respectively in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}/<(1,...,1)>
, correspond precisely to
partitions
, that is, weakly decreasing vectors in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
with non-negative entries, respectively to weakly decreasing vectors in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, and each such representative is equivalent to a unique partition in
\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, where the last entry is fixed as zero. An important property of normal crystals
B
is the existence of a unique highest weight vertex for each connected component of
B
, that is, an element which is a source in the corresponding crystal graph, whose weight is dominant. In
B
(λ)
, the highest weight vertex
x
has weight
\textsf{wt}(x)=\lambda
. Note that we work solely with
highest weight crystals
, namely, crystals
B
such that for each
b\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{B}{{{}}\textsf{B}}
, there exists a finite sequence
a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{l}\in I
and a highest weight element
u_{b}\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{B}{{{}}\textsf{B}}(b)
such that
b=f_{a_{l}}\cdots f_{a_{2}}f_{a_{1}}(u_{b})
. For
b,b^{\prime}\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{B}{{{}}\textsf{B}}
, we have
B(b)=B(b^{\prime})
if and only if
u_{b}=u_{b^{\prime}}
.
From now on, we will refer to
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})
-crystals by
C_{n}
-crystals, and
\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C})
-crystals by
A_{n-1}
-crystals.

4.1.
Kashiwara–Nakashima tableaux

Let
\textsf{B}(\lambda)
be the irreducible
C_{n}
-crystal with highest weight a partition
\lambda
of at most
n
parts.
We realize
\textsf{B}(\lambda)
as the crystal
\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)
of Kashiwara–Nakashima tableaux
[
kn91
]
of shape
\lambda
on the alphabet

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Corderedletters}{{{}}\mathcal{C}_{n}}=\{1<\cdots<n<\bar{n}<\cdots<\bar{1}\}.

The irreducible
A_{n-1}
-crystal with highest weight a partition
\lambda
of at most
n
parts is realized as the crystal
SSYT
(λ,n)
of semi-standard tableaux of shape
\lambda
on the alphabet
[n]
. We also will refer to these tableaux as the
A_{n-1}
tableaux of shape
\lambda
. The crystal
SSYT
(λ,n)
is a connected sub-crystal of
KN
(λ,n)
. The
weight
of an
A_{n-1}
tableau
T
, respectively a Kashiwara–Nakashima tableau
U
, is represented by, respectively is, the vector
(\mu_{1},...,\mu_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, where
\mu_{i}
denotes the number of
i
’s in
T
, respectively the number of
i
’s minus the number of
\bar{i}
’s in
U
.

Kashiwara–Nakashima tableaux (KN for short) are semi-standard Young tableaux in the alphabet
\mathcal{C}_{n}
which satisfy some extra conditions. They are a variation of De Concini symplectic tableaux
[
deconcini
]
. A semi-standard Young tableau of any shape (skew or straight) with entries in
\mathcal{C}_{n}
is KN if and only if the following two conditions hold.

•

Each one of its columns is
admissible.

•

Its splitting is a semi-standard Young tableau.

Definition 1
.

Let
C
be a semi-standard column in the alphabet
\mathcal{C}_{n}
of length at most
n
. Let
Z=\left\{z_{1}>...>z_{m}\right\}
be the set of non-barred letters
z
in
C

n

such that both
z
and
\bar{z}
appear in
C
. We say that the column
C
is admissible if there exists a set
T=\left\{t_{1}>...>t_{m}\right\}
of unbarred letters
t
that satisfies:

•

t,\bar{t}\notin C
;

•

t_{1}<z_{1}
and is maximal with this property;

•

t_{i}<min(t_{i-1},z_{i})
and is maximal with this property.

The split of a column is the two-column tableau
lCrC
where
lC
is the column obtained from
C
by replacing
z_{i}
by
t_{i}
and possibly re-ordering, and
rC
is obtained from
C
by replacing
\bar{z}_{i}
by
\bar{t}_{i}
and possibly re-ordering. The splitting of a tableau consisting of admissible columns is the concatenation of the splits of its columns.

Given
\mu\subseteq\lambda
partitions with at most
n
parts,
\textsf{KN}(\lambda/\mu,n)
denotes the normal
C_{n}
-crystal of KN
tableaux of skew shape
\lambda/\mu
on the alphabet
\mathcal{C}_{n}

[
lec02
, Lemma 6.1.3, Corollary 6.3.9]
.

Example 3
.

Let
n=2
. The column

2

\bar{2}

is admissible, however,

1

\bar{1}

is not. Notice that although each one of its columns is admissible, the tableau

2

2

\bar{2}

\bar{2}

is not KN, because its split,

1

2

1

2

\bar{2}

\bar{1}

\bar{2}

\bar{1}

is not semi-standard.

We will mostly use the notation and definitions from
[
lec02
,
lec07
]
. We also refer the reader to the references therein.

Remark 2
.

[
lec02
, Remark 2.2.2]

The maximal height of an admissible column is
n
. Moreover, a column
C
is admissible if and only if, for any
m\in[n]
,
the number
N(m)
of letters
x
in
C
such that either
x\leq m
or
x\geq\bar{m}
satisfies
N(m)\leq m
. Moreover, if there exists in
C
a letter
m\leq n
such that
N(m)>m
, then
C
contains a pair
(z,\bar{z})
satisfying
N(z)>z
.

Remark 3
.

In
[
lec02
]
,
coadmissible
columns are defined as well (see
[
lec02
, p.301]
). We will not delve into the details here, however, we remark that there exists a bijection between admissible and coadmissible columns given by filling in the shape of the given admissible column
C
with the unbarred letters of
lC
from top to bottom in increasing order, followed by the barred letters of
rC
in the same fashion. We will denote this bijection by
\Phi
and use it in
7.3
.

Example 4
.

The
C_{2}
crystal
\textsf{KN}(\lambda,2)
of shape
\lambda=(2,1)
. Each node in the graph represents an element of the crystal. There is a blue, respectively red, arrow connecting an element
a
to an element
b
whenever
f_{1}(a)=b
, respectively
f_{2}(a)=b
.

4.1.1.
Levi branching of KN tableau crystals

For
J\subseteq I,\hbox{ }\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{branchedsymptab}{{{}}\textsf{KN}_{J}(\lambda,n)}
is the restriction of
\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)
to the sub-diagram
J
of
I
: as a crystal graph it has the same set of vertices as
\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)
but only contains the arrows labelled by
J
, and it is also a normal crystal. The highest weight elements of
\textsf{KN}_{J}(\lambda,n)
are those
C_{n}
tableaux in
\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)
where the only incoming edges are colored in
[n]\setminus J
.

Example 5
.

We have the Levi-branched crystals
\textsf{KN}_{\left\{2\right\}}(\lambda,2)
and
\textsf{KN}_{\left\{1\right\}}(\lambda,2)
respectively from left to right for
\lambda=(2,1)
. Both are
A_{1}
-crystals. The highest weights, with multiplicity, in the LHS have representatives
(2,1),(1,2),(1,0)
,
(0,1),(0,1),(-1,2)
,
(-1,0),(-2,1)
. In the quotient of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
by the fundamental weight
\omega_{1}=(1,0)
, these are equivalent to the vectors
(0,1),(0,2),(0,0)
,
(0,1),(0,1),(0,2),(0,0),(0,1)
, respectively. In practice, this means that we have ignored the multiplicity of the letters
\{1,-1\}
in the tableaux of the LHS to compute the highest weights. On the RHS, we consider another embedding of
\mathbb{Z}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{2}
given by the quotient
\mathbb{Z}^{2}/<(1,1)>
, since
\omega_{2}=(1,1)
. The computation of the highest weights on the RHS is similar to that of the LHS, and we thus leave it as an exercise for the reader.

If
J=[p,q]
,
1\leq p<q\leq n
, the crystal graph
\textsf{KN}_{J}(\lambda,n)
consists of the KN tableaux of
\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)
with arrows colored
in
J
. Recall the
C_{n}
signature rule
[
kn91
,
lec02
,
BSch17
]
to compute the action of the crystal operators on a word in the alphabet
C

n

.

If
q<n
, the Levi branched crystal
\textsf{KN}_{[p,q]}(\lambda,n)
is a type

A_{q-p+1}
normal crystal. The Weyl group is
W^{J}=\mathfrak{S}_{[p,q+1]}
, the symmetric group on the letters
\{p,\dots,q+1\}
and
generators
r_{j}=(j,j+1)(\bar{j},\overline{j+1}),\hbox{ }j\in J.

We say that the entries outside of

[\pm p,q+1]=\{p<\dots<q+1\}\cup\{\overline{q+1}<\cdots<\overline{p}\}
are
frozen
, which amounts to saying that the KN tableaux of the set
\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)
in the same connected component of
\textsf{KN}_{[p,q]}(\lambda,n)
are stable in the entries over
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Corderedletters}{{{}}\mathcal{C}_{n}}\setminus[\pm p,q+1]
under the action of the Kashiwara operators
f_{i}
,
e_{i}
,
i\in[p,q]
. That is, if
q<n
, in the same
connected component of
\textsf{KN}_{[p,q]}(\lambda,n)
, the subtableaux consisting of the letters
\{1<\cdots<p-1\}
,
\{\overline{p-1}<\cdots<\overline{1}\}
or
\{{q+2}<\cdots<n<\bar{n}<\cdots<\overline{q+2}\}
are the same.

If
q=n,
the Levi
branched crystal

\textsf{KN}_{[p,n]}(\lambda,n)
is isomorphic to a type
C_{n-p+1}
normal crystal. The Weyl group is
W^{J}=B_{[p,n]}
generated
by the signed permutations on the subset
\{p<\dots<n<\bar{n}<\dots<\bar{i}\}
. The entries outside of

[\pm p,n]=\{p<\dots<n<\overline{n}<\cdots<\overline{p}\}
are
frozen
; within the same connected component of
\textsf{KN}_{J}(\lambda,n)
, the subtableaux either consisting of the letters
\{1<\cdots<p-1\}
or
\{\overline{p-1}<\cdots<\overline{1}\}
are the same.
In Example
5
, since
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})=\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{C})
, we get two crystals of types
A_{1}=C_{2}
.

5.
Virtualization

In this section we closely follow Baker
[
ba00a
, Section 2]
and adopt the notation used there. In Example
, we present a detailed example of the content in this section. We include it later rather than earlier because it includes some more information which is not
yet presented up to the end of this section.

5.1.
Baker embedding and Baker recording tableau

Let

\lambda=\lambda_{1}\omega_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{n}\omega_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}

with
\omega_{j}=\sum^{j}_{i=1}e_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,
1\leq j\leq n
the fundamental weights of type
C_{n}
. Let

\displaystyle\omega^{A}_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum^{j}_{i=1}e_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2n}\hbox{ for }1\leq j\leq n

(8)

\displaystyle\omega^{A}_{\bar{j}}=\omega^{A}_{2n-j+1}

\displaystyle=\sum^{2n-j+1}_{i=1}e_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2n}\hbox{ for }1<j\leq n

(9)

be the
A_{2n-1}
fundamental weights, and consider as well the
\mathbb{Z}^{2n}
partition

\lambda^{A}=2\lambda_{n}\omega^{A}_{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\lambda_{i}(\omega^{A}_{i}+\omega^{A}_{\overline{i+1}}).

Let
\textsf{SSYT}(\lambda^{A},n,\bar{n})
be the type
A_{2n-1}
crystal of semi-standard Young tableaux in the alphabet
C

n

of shape
\lambda^{A}
. We will denote the corresponding crystal operators
by
f^{A}_{i}
for
i\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Corderedletters}{{{}}\mathcal{C}_{n}}
and consider, for
1\leq i\leq n
, the operators

f^{E}_{i}=f^{A}_{i}f^{A}_{\overline{i+1}}
,
i<n
, and
f^{E}_{n}=(f^{A}_{n})^{2}
.
Let
E
denote the
virtualization map
defined on type
C_{n}
Kashiwara–Nakashima tableaux defined by Baker
[
ba00a
, Proposition 2.2, Proposition 2.3]
.
More precisely,
E
is an injective map

\displaystyle\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{E}{{{}}E}:\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{symptab}{{{}}\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)}\hookrightarrow\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{ssytv}{{{}}\textsf{SSYT}(\lambda^{A},n,\bar{n})}

(10)

such that
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{E}{{{}}E}(f_{i}(T))=f^{E}_{i}(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{E}{{{}}E}(T))
for
T\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{symptab}{{{}}\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)},1\leq i\leq n
. We will denote by
E^{-1}
the restriction of any left inverse of
E
to the image of
KN
(λ,n)
under
E
.

Given an admissible column
C
in the alphabet
C

n

of shape
\omega_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, denote by
\psi(C)
its

Baker virtual split

[
ba00a
, Proposition 2.2]
, a two column type
A_{2n-1}
tableau of shape
\omega^{A}_{i}+\omega^{A}_{2n-i}
. The map
\psi
is injective and embeds admissible columns of length
i
, in the alphabet
C

n

, into
\textsf{SSYT}(\omega^{A}_{i}+\omega^{A}_{2n-i},n,\bar{n}),1\leq i\leq n
. We define
\psi^{-1}
analogously to
E^{-1}
. From
[
ba00a
, Proposition 2.3]
we know that, if we write
T
as a concatenation of its columns, that is,
T=C_{k}\cdots C_{1}
,
then

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{E}{{{}}E}(T)=[\emptyset\leftarrow w(\psi(C_{1}))\leftarrow\cdots\leftarrow w(\psi(C_{k}))],

where the word
w(\psi(C))
of the type
A_{2n-1}
two-column tableau
\psi(C)
is given by the Japanese reading of its two columns (from top to bottom and right to left), and

P\leftarrow w
is the Schensted column insertion of a word
w
into a type
A_{2n-1}
semi-standard Young tableau
P
in the alphabet
C

n

[
Fu
,
Sta
]
.

Let
T_{\lambda}\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{symptab}{{{}}\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)}
be the highest weight element; that is,
T_{\lambda}
is the Yamanouchi tableau of shape and weight
\lambda
on the alphabet
[n]
(each row
i
is solely filled with the letter
i
). Then
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{E}{{{}}E}(T_{\lambda})=T_{\lambda^{A}}
is the highest weight element of
\textsf{SSYT}(\lambda^{A},n,\bar{n})
, that is, the
A_{2n-1}
Yamanouchi tableau of shape and weight
\lambda^{A}
in the alphabet
\mathcal{C}_{n}
. The image of
\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)
by
E
in
\textsf{SSYT}(\lambda^{A},n,\bar{n})
is the crystal generated by acting with the lowering operators
f^{E}_{i}
on
the highest weight element
T_{\lambda}^{A}
of
\textsf{SSYT}(\lambda^{A},n,\bar{n})
. For
T\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{symptab}{{{}}\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)}
, where
T=C_{k}\cdots C_{1}
, we write

w_{T}=w(\psi(C_{1}))\cdots w(\psi(C_{k})).

Then
w_{T}
is a word in
C

∗

n

, the monoid of words in the alphabet
C

n

, and
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{E}{{{}}E}(T)=[\emptyset\leftarrow w_{T}]
. We will call the
recording
tableau of the column insertion of
w_{T}
,
Q(w_{T})
, the
Baker recording tableau
associated to
T
.

Proposition 1
.

For
T\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{symptab}{{{}}\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)}
, the Baker recording tableau
Q(w_{T})
depends only on
\lambda
. From now on, we will denote by
Q_{\lambda}
the Baker recording tableau associated to
\lambda
.

Proof.

By abuse of notation, we will denote by the same symbols the type
A_{2n-1}
crystal operators on the
A_{2n-1}
crystal

C

∗

n

of words and those on semi-standard Young tableaux in the same alphabet. Now, we know that there exists
a sequence
1\leq i_{1},...,i_{k}\leq n
such that
f_{i_{k}}\cdots f_{i_{1}}(T_{\lambda})=T
. Therefore
f^{E}_{i_{k}}\cdots f^{E}_{i_{1}}(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{E}{{{}}E}(T_{\lambda}))=\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{E}{{{}}E}(T)
,
where
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{E}{{{}}E}(T_{\lambda})=T_{\lambda^{A}}
, the highest weight element of
SSYT
(λ

A

,n,
¯

n

)
, and so

f^{E}_{i_{k}}\cdots f^{E}_{i_{1}}(w_{T_{\lambda}})=w_{T}

(recall that
f^{E}_{n}=(f^{A}_{n})^{2}
) because the connected components of the
crystal
C

∗

n

of words of type
A_{2n-1}
with highest weight elements
w_{T_{\lambda}}
and
w(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{E}{{{}}E}(T_{\lambda}))=w(T_{\lambda^{A}})

have the same highest weight
\lambda^{A}
and are hence isomorphic. In particular, both
w_{T}
and
w_{T_{\lambda}}
belong to the same connected component of the crystal
C

∗

n

of words of type
A_{2n-1}
, namely, the connected component containing the Yamanouchi word
w_{T_{\lambda}}
of weight
\lambda^{A}
(recall that all words
w_{T}
have the same rectification shape
\lambda^{A}
and that all
A_{2n-1}
crystal operators commute with
jeu de taquin
). Now, we consider a version of the RSK correspondence
[
Fu
,
Sta
,
hoon
,
BSch17
]

which is a bijection

C

∗

n

\displaystyle\overset{1:1}{\longleftrightarrow}\underset{\begin{smallmatrix}\mu\\
\ell(\mu)\leq 2n\end{smallmatrix}}{\bigcup}\textsf{SSYT}(\mu,n,\bar{n})\times\textsf{SYT}(\mu)

(11)

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle\overset{\operatorname{RSK}}{\mapsto}(P(w),Q(w))

(12)

where
\textsf{SYT}(\mu)
is the set of standard Young tableaux of shape
\mu,P(w)=[\emptyset\leftarrow w]
and
Q(w)
is the corresponding recording tableau which encodes the sequence of shapes produced by the column insertion of
w
. In particular for each standard Young tableau
Q
of shape
\mu
the pre-image
\operatorname{RSK}^{-1}(\textsf{SSYT}(\mu,n,\bar{n})\times\{Q\})
is a crystal isomorphic to
\textsf{SSYT}(\mu,n,\bar{n})
, and all of these pre-images are disjoint and cover
C

∗

n

. In particular this means that all the words
w_{T}
for
T\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{symptab}{{{}}\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)}
are contained in the same connected component of
C

∗

n

defined by:

\operatorname{RSK}^{-1}(\hbox{\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{ssytv}{{{}}$\textsf{SSYT}(\lambda^{A},n,\bar{n})$}}\times\{Q(w_{T_{\lambda}})\}).

Thereby,
Q(w_{T})=Q(w_{T_{\lambda}})
for all
T\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{symptab}{{{}}\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)}
.
∎

Corollary 1
.

Let
\lambda=\omega_{m_{1}}+\cdots+\omega_{m_{k}}
,
1\leq m_{1}\leq\cdots\leq m_{k}\leq n
, and let

\lambda^{A}=\omega^{A}_{2n-m_{1}}+\omega^{A}_{m_{1}}+\cdots+\omega^{A}_{2n-m_{k}}+\omega^{A}_{m_{k}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2n}.

Then
Q_{\lambda}
can be written out of the shape
\lambda^{A}
as a sequence of shapes by adding successively the columns

\omega^{A}_{m_{1}},\omega^{A}_{2n-m_{1}},\dots,\omega^{A}_{m_{k}},\omega^{A}_{2n-m_{k}}
, whose boxes are filled along columns, top to bottom with
consecutive
numbers from 1 to
|\lambda^{A}|
:

\emptyset\subset\omega^{A}_{m_{1}}\subset\omega^{A}_{2n-m_{1}}+\omega_{m_{1}}\subset\omega^{A}_{m_{2}}+\omega^{A}_{2n-m_{1}}+\omega^{A}_{m_{1}}

\subset\omega^{A}_{2n-m_{2}}+\omega^{A}_{m_{2}}+\omega^{A}_{2n-m_{1}}+\omega^{A}_{m_{1}}

\subset\cdots\subset\omega^{A}_{m_{k}}+\cdots+\omega^{A}_{2n-m_{2}}+\omega^{A}_{m_{2}}+\omega^{A}_{2n-m_{1}}+\omega^{A}_{m_{1}}

\subset\omega^{A}_{2n-m_{k}}+\omega^{A}_{m_{k}}+\cdots+\omega^{A}_{2n-m_{1}}+\omega^{A}_{m_{1}}=\lambda^{A}.

Given a partition
\lambda
with at most
n
parts, and
T=C_{k}\cdots C_{1}\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{symptab}{{{}}\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)}
, let
\displaystyle\Psi(T)=

(w(\psi(C_{1})),

\ldots,w(\psi(C_{k})))\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Cstar}{{{}}\mathcal{C}^{*}_{n}}
(here the word is presented as a
k
-tuple)
and
\Psi^{-1}=\underbrace{(\psi^{-1},\dots,\psi^{-1})}_{k}
. Then
(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{E}{{{}}E}(T),Q_{\lambda})=

\textrm{RSK}\Psi(T)=(P(w_{T}),Q_{\lambda})
and

E^{-1}=\Psi^{-1}\textrm{RSK}^{-1}_{\mid\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{E}{{{}}E}(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{symptab}{{{}}\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)})\times\{Q_{\lambda}\}}

where

\textrm{RSK}^{-1}_{\mid\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{symptab}{{{}}\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)}\times\{Q_{\lambda}\}}
denotes the inverse of
RSK

restricted to
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{E}{{{}}E}(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{symptab}{{{}}\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)})\times\{Q_{\lambda}\}
.

The computation of
\textrm{RSK}^{-1}_{\mid\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{symptab}{{{}}\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)}\times\{Q_{\lambda}\}}
uses
Q_{\lambda}
to perform the inverse of column Schensted insertion. See Example
.

Remark 4
.

Let

5.2.
The Levi branched crystal and virtualization

Recall that a Levi branched crystal

J

,

a Dynkin diagram,

, for

. Let

1

n
-n

For each connected sub-diagram

, of

Each connected component of the Levi branched crystal

Consider the Levi branching of the type

.

In

and similarly for

and

. This means that

of

J

(λ,n)
if and only if, for all

,

and

\varepsilon_{n}^{A}(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{E}{{{}}E}(b))=\varepsilon_{n}^{C}(b)=0,\;\text{if $n\in J$}.

(Similarly, in the case

and

Henceforth,

\varepsilon_{i}^{C}(b)=0,\,i\in J\Leftrightarrow\varepsilon_{i}^{A}(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{E}{{{}}E}(b))=0,\;i\in J\cup\bar{J}

and

\varphi_{i}^{C}(b)=0,\,i\in J\Leftrightarrow\varphi_{i}^{A}(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{E}{{{}}E}(b))=0,\;i\in J\cup\bar{J}.

In other words, because our crystals are seminormal,
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{E}{{{}}E}(b)
is the highest weight element of the connected component

of

\textsf{SSYT}_{J\cup\bar{J}}(\lambda,n)
containing

and
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{E}{{{}}E}(U)
. It is
therefore unique. A similar statement holds for the lowest weight element. The next proposition now easily follows.

Proposition 2
.

Let

Remark 5
.

Those crystal operators preserve the shape decomposition above because, according to the type

For

\displaystyle f^{A}_{j_{r}}\cdots f^{A}_{{j_{1}}}f^{A}_{{j^{\prime}_{m}}}\cdots f^{A}_{{j^{\prime}_{1}}}(T)=(f^{A}_{j_{r}}\cdots f^{A}_{{j_{1}}}(T^{+}),f^{A}_{{j^{\prime}_{m}}}\cdots f^{A}_{{j^{\prime}_{1}}}(T^{-})).

Remark 6
.

Note that when

Lemma 1

The cactus group

s_{[p,q]}s_{[k,l]}=s_{[p+q-l,p+q-k]}s_{[p,q]}

Proposition 3
.

If

Let

, and generators of

. Remark

implies that this map is a morphism of groups. The map is clearly injective because the generators of

are all distinct.

\displaystyle=1\hbox{, }1\leq p\leq q<n,

(19)

\displaystyle s^{2}_{[p,2n-p]}

\displaystyle=1\hbox{, }1\leq p<n,

(20)

\displaystyle s^{\prime}_{[p,q]}s^{\prime}_{[k,l]}

\displaystyle=s^{\prime}_{[k,l]}s^{\prime}_{[p,q]}\hbox{, }\text{ $[p,q]\cup[k,l]\subseteq[n-1]$ disconnected},

(21)

\displaystyle s_{[p,2n-p]}s_{[k,2n-k]}

\displaystyle=s_{[k,2n-k]}s_{[p,2n-p]}\hbox{, }1\leq p<k<n,

(22)

\displaystyle s_{[p,2n-p]}s^{\prime}_{[k,l]}

\displaystyle=s^{\prime}_{[k,l]}s_{[p,2n-p]}\hbox{, }1\leq p\leq k\leq l<n,

(23)

\displaystyle s^{\prime}_{[p,q]}s^{\prime}_{[k,l]}

\displaystyle=s^{\prime}_{[p+q-l,p+q-k]}s^{\prime}_{[p,q]}\hbox{, }1\leq p\leq k\leq l\leq q<n.

(24)

There are no more relations among the elements
s^{\prime}_{[p,q]}
and
s_{[k,2n-k]}
, for all
[p,q]\subseteq[n-1]
and
[k,n]\subseteq[n]
.

Proof.

We have the relations (
19
), (
20
) and (
23
),

{s^{\prime}_{[p,q]}}^{2}=(s_{[p,q]}s_{[2n-q,2n-p]})^{2}=s^{2}_{[p,q]}s^{2}_{[2n-q,2n-p]}\overset{1A.}{=}1.

s^{2}_{[p,2n-p]}\overset{1A.}{=}1.

For
1\leq p\leq q<n
and
1\leq k\leq l<n
such that
[p,q]\cup[k,l]
is disconnected,
the sub-diagrams
[p,q]\cup[2n-q,2n-p]
, and
[k,l]\cup[2n-l,2n-k]
of
[2n-1]
are disconnected, hence

s^{\prime}_{[p,q]}s^{\prime}_{[k,l]}\overset{2A.}{=}s^{\prime}_{[k,l]}s^{\prime}_{[p,q]}.

Additionally, if
q=n
, the sub-diagram
[k,l]\cup[p,2n-p]\cup[2n-l,2n-k]
in
[2n-1]
is disconnected, hence

s_{[p,2n-p]}s^{\prime}_{[k,l]}\overset{2A.}{=}s^{\prime}_{[k,l]}s_{[p,2n-p]}.

Moreover,

s_{[p,2n-p]}s_{[k,2n-k]}\overset{3A.}{=}s_{[2n-(2n-k),2n-k]}s_{[p,2n-p]}=s_{[k,2n-k]}s_{[p,2n-p]}

for
1\leq p<k<n
, hence relation (
22
) holds. Now, for
1\leq p<k<l<n
we have:

\displaystyle s_{[p,2n-p]}s_{[k,l]}s_{[2n-l,2n-k]}\overset{3A.}{=}

\displaystyle s_{[2n-l,2n-k]}s_{[p,2n-p]}s_{[2n-l,2n-k]}

\displaystyle\overset{3A.}{=}

\displaystyle s_{[2n-l,2n-k]}s_{[2n-(2n-k),2n-(2n-l)]}s_{[p,2n-p]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{[2n-l,2n-k]}s_{[k,l]}s_{[p,2n-p]}

which establishes relation (
23
). Finally, for
1\leq p<k<l<q<n
the following holds:

\displaystyle s^{\prime}_{[p,q]}s^{\prime}_{[k,l]}=

\displaystyle s_{[p,q]}s_{[2n-q,2n-p]}s_{[k,l]}s_{[2n-l,2n-k]}

\displaystyle\overset{2A.}{=}

\displaystyle s_{[p,q]}s_{[k,l]}s_{[2n-q,2n-p]}s_{[2n-l,2n-k]}

\displaystyle\overset{3A.}{=}

\displaystyle s_{[p+q-l,p+q-k]}s_{[p,q]}s_{[2n-(p+q-k),2n-(p+q-l)]}s_{[2n-q,2n-p]}

\displaystyle\overset{2A.}{=}

\displaystyle s_{[p+q-l,p+q-k]}s_{[2n-(p+q-k),2n-(p+q-l)]}s_{[p,q]}s_{[2n-q,2n-p]}

\displaystyle=s^{\prime}_{[p+q-l,p+q-k]}s^{\prime}_{[p,q]}.

This establishes relation (
24
).
Any relation
R^{\prime}=1
with the elements
s^{\prime}_{[p,q]}=s_{[p,q]}s_{[2n-q,2n-p]}
and
s_{[k,2n-k]}
, for some
[p,q]\subseteq[n-1]
and

[k,n]\subseteq[n]
, translates to a relation
R=1
involving generators of
J_{2n}
, of the form
s_{[p,q]}
,
s_{[2n-q,2n-p]}
in pairs,
and
s_{[k,2n-k]}
, for some
[p,q]\subseteq[n-1]
and
[k,n]\subseteq[n]
, which satisfy the same kind of relations as
s^{\prime}_{[p,q]}
and

s_{[k,2n-k]}
. Therefore from
R=1
we don’t get new relations
R^{\prime}=1
.
∎

Definition 3
.

The virtual symplectic cactus group
\widetilde{J}_{2n}
is the group with generators
\tilde{s}_{J}
, where
J
runs over all sub-diagrams of
I=[2n-1]
, the
A_{2n-1}
Dynkin diagram, of the form
J=[p,2n-p]
for all
[p,n]\subseteq[n]
, or
J=[p,q]\cup[2n-q,2n-p]
for all
[p,q]\subseteq[n-1]
subject to the relations

1
\tilde{A}
.

\tilde{s}_{J}^{2}=1,J\subseteq[2n-1]
,

2
\tilde{A}
.

\tilde{s}_{J}\tilde{s}_{J^{\prime}}=\tilde{s}_{J^{\prime}}\tilde{s}_{J},
such that
J\cup J^{\prime}
is disconnected with respect to all
[p,q]\subseteq[n]
,

3
\tilde{A}
.

(i)

\tilde{s}_{[p,2n-p]}\tilde{s}_{[q,l]\cup[2n-l,2n-q]}=\tilde{s}_{[q,l]\cup[2n-l,2n-q]}\tilde{s}_{[p,2n-p]},[q,l]\subseteq[p,n]\subseteq[n]
,

(ii)

for
[k,l]\subseteq[p,q]\subseteq[n-1]
,

\displaystyle\tilde{s}_{[p,q]\cup[2n-q,2n-p]}\tilde{s}_{[k,l]\cup[2n-l,2n-k]}=

\displaystyle\tilde{s}_{[q+p-l,q+p-k]\cup[2n-p+2n-q-(2n-k),2n-p+2n-q-(2n-l)]}\tilde{s}_{[p,q]\cup[2n-q,2n-p]}=

\displaystyle\tilde{s}_{[q+p-l,q+p-k]\cup[2n-(p+q)+k,2n-(p+q)+l]}\tilde{s}_{[p,q]\cup[2n-q,2n-p]}.

The following are
2n-1
alternative generators of
\widetilde{J}_{2n}
:

\displaystyle\tilde{s}_{[1,j]\cup[2n-j,2n-1]},\;1\leq j\leq n-1,

(25)

\displaystyle\tilde{s}_{[j,2n-j]},\;1\leq j\leq n.

(26)

Proposition 4
.

There is an isomorphism
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{vJ2n}{{{}}\widetilde{J}_{2n}}\simeq\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Jsp}{{{}}J_{\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})}}
.

Proof.

Clearly

~

J

2
n

and
J

s
p
(
2
n
,
C
)

satisfy the same relations corresponding to all connected sub-diagrams
[p,q]\subseteq[n]
. Furthermore, the maps

J

s
p
(
2
n
,
C
)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{vJ2n}{{{}}\widetilde{J}_{2n}}

\displaystyle s_{[p,q]}

\displaystyle\mapsto\tilde{s}_{[p,q]\cup[2n-q,2n-p]},

\displaystyle s_{[p,n]}

\displaystyle\mapsto\tilde{s}_{[p,2n-p]},

~

J

2
n

\displaystyle\rightarrow\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Jsp}{{{}}J_{\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})}}

\displaystyle\tilde{s}_{[p,q]\cup[2n-q,2n-p]}

\displaystyle\mapsto s_{[p,q]},

\displaystyle\tilde{s}_{[p,2n-p]}

\displaystyle\mapsto s_{[p,n]}

are epimorphisms inverse to each other. This follows directly from the definitions of

~

J

2
n

and
J

s
p
(
2
n
,
C
)

(Definition
3
and Lemma
2
respectively). Therefore,
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Jsp}{{{}}J_{\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})}}\simeq\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{vJ2n}{{{}}\widetilde{J}_{2n}}
.
∎

Lemma 4
.

The following assignment defines a group injection from
J

s
p
(
2
n
,
C
)

to
J_{2n}
:

\begin{array}[]{cccccc}\Gamma:&\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Jsp}{{{}}J_{\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})}}&\hookrightarrow&J_{2n}\\
&s_{[p,q]}&\mapsto&s^{\prime}_{[p,q]},&1\leq p\leq q<n,\\
&s_{[p,n]}&\mapsto&s_{[p,2n-p]},&1\leq p\leq n.\end{array}

Proof.

We begin by showing that the map induced by
\Gamma
is indeed a group homomorphism. We check the relations
1C.-3C.
from Lemma

2
.

1C.

We have that for
1\leq p\leq q<n,

\Gamma({s_{[p,q]}}^{2})={s^{\prime}_{[p,q]}}^{2}\overset{\eqref{cactisymm01}}{=}1,

while for
1\leq p<n
, we have
\Gamma({s_{[p,n]}}^{2})=s^{2}_{[p,2n-p]}\overset{\eqref{cactisymm002}}{=}1
.

2C.

For
1\leq p\leq q<n
and
1\leq k\leq l<n
such that
[p,q]\cup[k,l]
is disconnected,
the sub-diagrams
[p,q]\cup[2n-q,2n-p]
, and
[k,l]\cup[2n-l,2n-k]
of
[2n-1]
are disconnected, hence

\Gamma(s_{[p,q]}s_{[k,l]}){=}s^{\prime}_{[p,q]}s^{\prime}_{[k,l]}\overset{\eqref{cactisymm02}}{=}s^{\prime}_{[k,l]}s^{\prime}_{[p,q]}=\Gamma(s_{[k,l]}s_{[p,q]}).

Additionally, if
q=n
, the sub-diagram
[k,l]\cup[p,2n-p]\cup[2n-l,2n-k]
in
[2n-1]
is disconnected, hence

\Gamma(s_{[p,n]}s_{[k,l]}){=}s_{[p,2n-p]}s^{\prime}_{[k,l]}\overset{\eqref{cactisymm2}}{=}s^{\prime}_{[k,l]}s_{[p,2n-p]}{=}\Gamma(s_{[k,l]}s_{[p,n]}).

3C.

(i)

We have that for
1\leq p<k<n
and
1\leq p<k<l<n
respectively:

\displaystyle\Gamma(s_{[p,n]}s_{[k,n]})=s_{[p,2n-p]}s_{[k,2n-k]}

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{cactisymm1})}{=}s_{[k,2n-k]}s_{[p,2n-p]}=\Gamma(s_{[k,n]}s_{[p,n]})

\displaystyle\Gamma(s_{[p,n]}s_{[k,l]})=s_{[p,2n-p]}s_{[k,l]}s_{[2n-l,2n-k]}

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{cactisymm2})}{=}s_{[2n-l,2n-k]}s_{[k,l]}s_{[p,2n-p]}=\Gamma(s_{[k,l]}s_{[p,n]}).

(ii)

Let
1\leq p<k<l<q<n
. Then

\Gamma(s_{[p,q]}s_{[k,l]})=s^{\prime}_{[p,q]}s^{\prime}_{[k,l]}\overset{(\ref{cactisymm3})}{=}s^{\prime}_{[p+q-l,p+q-k]}s^{\prime}_{[p,q]}=\Gamma(s_{[p+q-l,p+q-k]}s_{[p,q]}).

We have now finished proving that
\Gamma
is a group morphism. To show that it is injective, one needs to show that its left
inverse defined by the assignment

\begin{array}[]{cccccc}\Gamma^{-1}_{left}:&\operatorname{im}(\Gamma)\subset J_{2n}&\hookrightarrow&\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Jsp}{{{}}J_{\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})}}\\
&s^{\prime}_{[p,q]}&\mapsto&s_{[p,q]},&1\leq p\leq q<n,\\
&s_{[p,2n-p]}&\mapsto&s_{[p,n]},&1\leq p\leq n.\end{array}

is also a group morphism. This however follows from Lemma
3
the previous calculations: the generators of

\operatorname{im}(\Gamma)
satisfy the relations from Lemma
2
, and there are no more relations between them (all possible cases have
been already covered above).
∎

Proposition 5
.

The group

~

J

2
n

is isomorphic to a subgroup of
J_{2n}
.

Proof.

The map

~

J

2
n

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow J_{2n}

\displaystyle\tilde{s}_{[p,q]\cup[2n-q,2n-p]}

\displaystyle\mapsto s^{\prime}_{[p,q]},1\leq p\leq q<n,

\displaystyle\tilde{s}_{[p,2n-p]}

\displaystyle\mapsto s_{[p,2n-p]},1\leq p\leq n.

is a a group injection. This follows directly after composing the maps from Proposition
4
and Lemma
4
.

∎

We may also think of

~

J

2
n

as the
unfolding
of
J

s
p
(
2
n
,
C
)

in
J_{2n}
.

7.
Full Schützenberger–Lusztig involutions and algorithms

7.1.
Full Schützenberger–Lusztig involution

Let
B
(λ)
be the normal
g
-crystal with
highest weight
\lambda
. Let
u_{\lambda}
and
u^{low}_{\lambda}
be the highest, respectively lowest, weight elements of
B
(λ)
.
The
Schützenberger–Lusztig involution
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}:\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{cncrystal}{{{}}\textsf{B}(\lambda)}\rightarrow\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{cncrystal}{{{}}\textsf{B}(\lambda)}
is the unique map of sets
(hence set involution)

such
that, for all
b\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{cncrystal}{{{}}\textsf{B}(\lambda)}
, and
i\in I
,

•

e_{i}\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}(b)=\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}f_{\theta(i)}(b)

•

f_{i}\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}(b)=\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}e_{\theta(i)}(b)

•

\textsf{wt}(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}(b))=w_{0}\textsf{wt(b)}

where
w_{0}
is the long element of the Weyl group
W
. ( For the existence and uniqueness of
\xi
,
\xi^{2}
is a map of crystals and
hence
\xi^{2}=1
, see
[
hk06
,
BSch17
]
.) The involution

ξ
acts by
w_{0}
on the weights and
interchanges the action of
e_{i}
and
f_{\theta(i)}
. For
A_{n-1}
,
ξ
acts by reversing the weight and interchanges the action of

e_{i}
and
f_{n-i}
; for
C_{n}
,
ξ
acts by changing the sign of the weight and interchanges the action of
e_{i}
and
f_{i}
.

If
B
is a normal
g
-crystal,
B
is the disjoint union of connected components, each of which is a crystal isomorphic to

B
(λ)
for some dominant integral weight
\lambda
. We define
ξ

B

on
B
by applying
ξ
to each one of its
connected components. Each element of
B
(λ)
is generated by
u_{\lambda}
(resp.
u_{\lambda}^{\textsf{low}}
) by applying
f_{i}
’s
(resp.
e_{i}
’s). Hence the same sequence of
f_{i}
’s (resp.
e_{i}
’s) applies to the highest weight (resp. lowest weight) of any connected
component of
B
isomorphic to
B
(λ)
.

The elements
u_{\lambda}
and
u_{\lambda}^{\textsf{low}}
are the unique elements of
B
(λ)
of weight
\lambda
, respectively

w_{0}\lambda
. Hence, since
\textsf{wt}(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}(u_{\lambda}))=w_{0}\lambda
, and
\textsf{wt}(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}(u^{\textsf{low}}_{\lambda}))=\lambda
,
\xi
interchanges highest and weight elements of
B(\lambda)
, and so

u_{\lambda}^{\textsf{low}}=\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}(u_{\lambda})
,
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}(u_{\lambda}^{\textsf{low}})=u_{\lambda}
. This implies that,

u_{\lambda}=e_{j_{r}}\cdots e_{j_{1}}(u_{\lambda}^{\textsf{low}})
, for some sequence
j_{1},\dots,j_{r}\in I
, and

u_{\lambda}^{\textsf{low}}=\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}(u_{\lambda})=\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}(e_{j_{r}}\cdots e_{j_{1}}(u_{\lambda}^{\textsf{low}}))=f_{\theta(j_{r})}\cdots f_{\theta(j_{1})}(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}u_{\lambda}^{\textsf{low}})=f_{\theta(j_{r})}\cdots f_{\theta(j_{1})}(u_{\lambda}).

Corollary 2
.

Let
b\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{cncrystal}{{{}}\textsf{B}(\lambda)}
and
b=f_{j_{r}}\cdots f_{j_{1}}(u_{\lambda})
, for
j_{r},\dots,j_{1}\in I
. Then

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}(b)=e_{\theta(j_{r})}\cdots e_{\theta(j_{1})}(u_{\lambda}^{\textsf{low}}),\quad\textsf{wt}(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}(b))=w_{0}\textsf{wt}(b)

In particular,

•

in type
A_{n-1}
,
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}(b)=e_{n-j_{r}}\cdots e_{n-j_{1}}(u_{\lambda}^{\textsf{low}}),
and

\textsf{wt}(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}(b))={rev}\,\textsf{wt}(b)
, where
rev
is the reverse permutation (long element) of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
,

•

in type
C_{n}
,
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}(b)=e_{j_{r}}\cdots e_{j_{1}}(u_{\lambda}^{\textsf{low}}),
and
\textsf{wt}(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}(b))=-\textsf{wt}(b).

7.2.
The full
\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C})
reversal

For
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C})
,
ξ
coincides with the
Schützenberger involution
[
lenart
,
bz96
]
also known as

evacuation

(
evac
for short) on
\textsf{SSYT}(\lambda,n)

[
Fu
,
Sta
]
, and as
reversal
on the set
\textsf{SSYT}(\lambda/\mu,n)
of type
A_{n-1}

tableaux of skew-shape
\lambda/\mu
in the alphabet
[n]

[
bss
]
.

Let
T\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{B}{{{}}\textsf{B}}=\textsf{SSYT}(\lambda/\mu,n)
and let
\textsf{B}(T)
be the connected component of the
crystal
\textsf{SSYT}(\lambda/\mu,n)
containing
T
. Then
\textsf{B}(T)\simeq\textsf{B}(\nu)
for some partition
\nu
and

\textsf{rectification}(T)\in\textsf{B}(\nu)
. Thereby,

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}(T)
is the unique tableau in
\textsf{B}(T)
such that

\textsf{rectification}\,\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}(T)=\textsf{evacuation}(\textsf{rectification}(T)),

\displaystyle\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}(T)=\textsf{arectification}(\textsf{evacuation}(\textsf{rectification}(T))),

(27)

where
arectification
denotes the inverse process of
rectification

[
bss
,
AzenhasConflittiMamede2019
]
. More
precisely, the
rectification
(
rect
for short) procedure is recorded by assigning to the inner shape
\mu
of
T
a
standard tableau

S
to form the tableau pair
(S,T)
. The entries of
S
govern the
jeu de taquin
on
T

by sliding out all letters in the
S
filling, from the largest to the smallest, to get a new tableau pair
(\textsf{rect}(T),S^{\prime})
where

S^{\prime}
is the skew standard tableau consisting of the slid letters from
S
. The anti-rectification procedure,
arectification
, is
defined by the reverse
jeu
de taquin
to
\textsf{evacuation}(\textsf{rectification}(T)
and is governed by the slid letters in
S^{\prime}
in
the tableau pair
(\textsf{evacuation}(\textsf{rectification}(T)),S^{\prime})
from the smallest to the largest. Eventually one obtains the tableau
pair
(S,\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{rev}{{{}}\textsf{reversal}}(T))
where

\displaystyle\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{rev}{{{}}\textsf{reversal}}(T):=\textsf{arectification}(\textsf{evacuation}(\textsf{rectification}(T))).

(28)

Next we will discuss
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})
.

7.3.
Lecouvey–Sheats symplectic jeu de taquin and symplectic Knuth equivalence

If
T
is a KN tableau, we consider its word
w(T)\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Cstar}{{{}}\mathcal{C}^{*}_{n}}
obtained by reading in the Japanese way the columns of
T
from rightmost to leftmost, each column read from top to bottom.

7.3.1.
Lecouvey–Sheats symplectic jeu de taquin

[
she99
,
lec02
]

Let
T
be a punctured KN tableau with two columns
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
and split form
spl(T)=lC_{1}rC_{1}lC_{2}rC_{2}
, and let
C_{1}
have the puncture
\ast
. Let
\alpha
be the entry under the puncture of
rC_{1}
and
\beta
the entry to the right of the puncture of
rC_{1}
,

spl(T)=lC_{1}rC_{1}lC_{2}rC_{2}=\vbox{\hbox{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{4.19969pt}{-58.14789pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 44 -88 M 44 -88 L 44 -66 L 22 -66 L 22 -88 Z M 22 -66}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 33 -77}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{20.09909pt}{-58.14789pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}},

where
\alpha
or
\beta
may not necessarily exist. The elementary steps of the symplectic jeu de taquin, or SJDT for short, are the following:

A.
If
\alpha\leq\beta
or
\beta
does not exist, then the puncture of
T
will change its position with the cell beneath it. This is a vertical slide.

B.
If the slide is not vertical, then it is horizontal. We then have
\alpha>\beta
or that
\alpha
does not exist. Let
C_{1}^{\prime}
and
C_{2}^{\prime}
be the columns obtained after the slide. We have two subcases, depending on the sign of
\beta
:

1. If
\beta
is barred, we are moving a barred letter,
\beta
, from
lC_{2}
to the punctured box of
rC_{1}
, and the puncture will occupy
\beta
’s place in
lC_{2}
. Note that
lC_{2}
has the same barred part as
C_{2}
and that
rC_{1}
has the same barred part as
\Phi(C_{1})
. Looking at
T
, we will have an horizontal slide of the puncture, getting
C_{2}^{\prime}=C_{2}\setminus\{\beta\}\sqcup\{\ast\}
and
C_{1}^{\prime}=\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(C_{1})\setminus{\ast}\sqcup\{\beta\})
. In a sense,
\beta
went from
C_{2}
to
\Phi(C_{1})
.

2. If
\beta
is unbarred, the procedure is similar, but this time
\beta
will go from
\Phi(C_{2})
to
C_{1}
; hence
C_{1}^{\prime}=C_{1}\setminus{\ast}\cup\{\beta\}
and
C_{2}^{\prime}=\Phi^{-1}(\Phi(C_{2})\setminus\{\beta\}\sqcup{\ast})
. However, in this case it may happen that
C_{1}^{\prime}
is no longer admissible. In this situation, if
i
is the lowest entry such that
i,\bar{i}
appear in
C_{1}^{\prime}
and
N(i)>i
, we erase both
i
and
\overline{i}
from the column and remove a cell from the bottom and from the top of the column, and place all the remaining cells orderly.

Applying elementary SJDT slides successively, eventually, the puncture will be a cell such that
\alpha
and
\beta
do not exist. In this case we redefine the shape to not include this cell and the
jeu de taquin
ends. The SJDT when applied to semi-standard tableaux in the alphabet
[n]
reduces to the ordinary
jeu de taquin
.

The SJDT is reversible, meaning that we can move
\ast
, the empty cell outside of
\mu
, to the inner shape
\nu
of a skew tableau
T
of shape
\mu/\nu
, simultaneously increasing both the inner and outer shapes of
T
by one cell. The slides work similarly to the previous case: the vertical slide means that an empty cell is going up, and a horizontal slide means that an entry goes from
\Phi(C_{1})
to
C_{2}
or from
C_{1}
to
\Phi(C_{2})
, depending on whether the slid entry is barred or not, respectively.

7.3.2.
Symplectic Knuth equivalence

In this section we gather the necessary tools from
[
LLT95
,
lec02
]
. For
w\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Cstar}{{{}}\mathcal{C}^{*}_{n}}
, let
P(w)
be the Kashiwara–Nakashima tableau obtained by performing the Baker–Lecouvey insertion algorithm on
w
. We do not need the algorithm in this paper, but refer the reader to
[
ba00b
,
lec02
]
for the original descriptions. A detailed account can also be found in
[
sa21b
]
. Given
w_{1},w_{2}\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Cstar}{{{}}\mathcal{C}^{*}_{n}}
, the relation
w_{1}\sim w_{2}\Leftrightarrow P(w_{1})=P(w_{2})
defines an equivalence relation on
C

∗

n

known as
symplectic plactic equivalence
. It is the analogous relation defined by Knuth relations in the alphabet
[n]

[
Fu
]
. The
symplectic plactic monoid
is the quotient
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Cstar}{{{}}\mathcal{C}^{*}_{n}}/\sim
. Each
symplectic plactic class
is uniquely identified with a KN tableau.

The plactic monoid
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Cstar}{{{}}\mathcal{C}^{*}_{n}}/\sim
can also be described as the quotient of
C_{n}^{\ast}
by the following
symplectic plactic relations
(we use the notation from
[
lec02
]
):

R1

\displaystyle yzx\cong yxz\hbox{ for }x\leq y<z\hbox{ with }z\neq\bar{x}

\displaystyle xzy\cong zxy\hbox{ for }x<y\leq z\hbox{ with }z\neq\bar{x}

R2

\displaystyle y\overline{x-1}(x-1)\cong yx\overline{x}\hbox{ and }x\overline{x}y\cong\overline{x-1}(x-1)y\hbox{ for }1<x\leq n\hbox{ and }x\leq y\leq\bar{x}

R3

(Symplectic contraction/dilation relation)

w\sim w\setminus\{z,\overline{z}\}
, where
w\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Cstar}{{{}}\mathcal{C}^{*}_{n}}
and
z\in[n]
are such that
w
is a non-admissible column,
z
is the lowest non-barred letter in
w
such that
N(z)=z+1
and any proper factor of
w
is an admissible column.

Remark 10
.

[
sa21a
]

It can be proven that given a column word
w\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Cstar}{{{}}\mathcal{C}^{*}_{n}}
, any proper factor is admissible if and only if any proper prefix of
w
is admissible. Thus, in order to be able to apply the plactic relation
\bf R3
to a non-admissible column word
w
, we need only check that all proper prefixes of
w
are admissible, instead of all proper factors. For example,

234\bar{4}\bar{3}\overset{\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{r3}{{{}}\bf R3}}{\equiv}23\bar{3},\quad 1234\bar{4}\bar{3}\overset{\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{r3}{{{}}\bf R3}}{\equiv}123\bar{3}\overset{\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{r3}{{{}}\bf R3}}{\equiv}12.

(29)

When Knuth relations are applied to factors of a word, the weight is preserved while the length may not be. Knuth relations can be seen as
jeu de taquin
moves on words or diagonally shaped tableaux, and each symplectic
jeu de taquin
slide preserves the Knuth class of the reading word of a tableau
[
lec02
, Theorem 6.3.8]
.
The words
23\overline{2}\overline{3}1
and
\overline{1}113\overline{3}
are Knuth related:

\overline{1}113\overline{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R1}}{{\sim}}\overline{1}131\overline{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R1}}{{\sim}}\overline{1}13\overline{3}1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R2}}{{\sim}}2\overline{2}3\overline{3}1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R1}}{{\sim}}23\overline{2}\overline{3}1
.

7.4.
Full symplectic reversal

7.4.1.
Symplectic evacuation algorithm

In
[
sa21a
]
, Santos introduced a symplectic evacuation algorithm on tableaux in
KN
(λ,n)
denoted by
\textsf{evac}^{C_{n}}
which he proved coincides with the full Lusztig–Schützenberger involution on a given
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C}))
-crystal
B
(λ)
associated to a representation of highest weight
\lambda
. The algorithm is defined on a given tableau
T\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{symptab}{{{}}\textsf{KN}(\lambda,n)}
as follows. First, one complements its entries, that is, replaces all unbarred
i
’s by
\bar{i}
’s and all
\bar{i}
’s by
i
’s (this amounts to the action of
w_{0}^{C}=-id
on the entries of the tableau). Second, one performs a rotation by
\pi
to obtain a skew tableau. Finally, one performs symplectic rectification or insertion using Lecouvey–Sheats symplectic jeu de taquin
[
she99
,
lec02
,
lec07
]
, or Baker–Lecouvey insertion
[
ba00b
,
lec02
,
lec07
]
respectively. The resulting tableau is defined to be
\textsf{evac}^{C_{n}}(T)
. We refer the reader to
[
sa21a
, Section 5]
for detailed examples of the algorithm. Santos’ evacuation mimics the Schützenberger
evacuation
on
SSYT
(λ,n)
by replacing the action of the long element of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
with that of the long element of
B_{n}
.

7.4.2.
Full symplectic reversal on KN skew tableaux

The set
\textsf{KN}(\lambda/\mu,m)
is a normal
C_{m}
crystal whose connected components are isomorphic to
\textsf{KN}(\nu,m)
for some partition
\nu
whose number of boxes
|\nu|
might be less than
|\lambda|-|\mu|
. Let
n=m+j-1
, where
1\leq j-1<n
is the number of parts of
\mu
and
J=[j,n]
. Shifting the entries of the skew KN tableaux in
\textsf{KN}(\lambda/\mu,m)
by
j-1
, we may identify
\textsf{KN}(\lambda/\mu,m)
with the (normal) full sub-crystal
\textsf{B}(\lambda,\mu)\subset\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{branchedsymptab}{{{}}\textsf{KN}_{J}(\lambda,n)}
consisting of the tableaux in
KN
(λ,n)
with entries exclusively in
1<\cdots<j<j+1<\cdots<j+m<\overline{j+m}<\cdots<\overline{j}
and whose sub-tableaux on the alphabet
\{1,\dots,j-1\}
is the fixed Yamanouchi tableau of shape
\mu

[
lec02
, Lemma 6.1.3]
.
\textsf{B}(\lambda,\mu)
is stable under the action of
f_{i+j-1},e_{i+j-1}
,
i=1,\dots,m
, and it decomposes into connected components of
KN

J

(λ,n)
. That is, the crystal operators,
f_{i},e_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,m
do not change the skew-shape of a KN tableau on the alphabet
\mathcal{C}_{m}
, and
\textsf{KN}(\lambda/\mu,m)
decomposes into connected components that can be identified with the connected components of
\textsf{B}(\mu,\lambda)
.

In both type
A_{n-1}
and type
C_{n}
, Kashiwara operators
e_{i}
and
f_{i}
commute with SJDT slides. Let
T\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{B}{{{}}\textsf{B}}=\textsf{KN}(\lambda/\mu,n)
. An inner corner in
T
is a box of
\mu
such that the boxes below and to the right are not in
\mu
; an outer corner in
T
is a box of
\lambda
such that the boxes below and to the right are not in
\lambda
. Let
c
be a fixed inner/outer corner of
T
. An
SJDT slide
or a
complete SJDT slide
to the inner corner
c
means a slide of the box
c
from an inner corner to an outer corner, or vice-versa. An SJDT slide to the inner/outer corner
c
of
T
gives a new KN skew tableau
SJDT(T,c)
, possibly with fewer/more boxes. Applying an SJDT slide to the same inner corner
c
in all vertices of
\textsf{B}(T)
defines an isomorphic crystal
\textsf{B}(SJDT(T,c))

[
lec02
, Theorem 6.3.8]
. The images of the KN tableaux in the same connected component of
\textsf{KN}(\lambda/\mu,m)
under this crystal isomorphism have the same skew shape
[
lec02
, Theorem 6.3.8]
. Iterating the SJDT to all inner corners of
T
rectifies
T
, producing
\textsf{rect}(T)

[
she99
, Proposition 9.2]
,
[
lec02
, Theorem 6.1.9, Theorem 6.3.9]
.

At the end of each SJDT slide, the inner corner (outer corner) where the slide started is filled, or the column where the slide started has 2 fewer (more) boxes
[
she99
, Proposition 9.2]
,
[
lec02
, Theorem 6.1.9]
. The SJDT step where the tableau loses two boxes in a column has a previous step where this column is non- admissible but Knuth equivalent to the new column which is admissible. The step in reverse SJDT where the tableau gains two boxes in a column is
R3
Knuth equivalent to the previous one which is admissible. Therefore, in each step of SJDT we get crystals which are isomorphic. This allows, in the vein of reversal for
A_{n-1}
skew semi-standard tableaux, the definition of symplectic reversal,
reversal

C

n

, on type
C_{n}
skew tableaux as a coplactic extension of
\textsf{evacuation}^{C_{n}}
.

Lemma 5
.

Let
T\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{B}{{{}}\textsf{B}}=\textsf{KN}(\lambda/\mu,n)
.
Then
\xi^{C_{n}}(T)
is the unique KN tableau in
\textsf{B}(T)
that is symplectic Knuth equivalent to
\textsf{evac}^{C_{n}}\,\textsf{rect}(T)
, and

\displaystyle\textsf{rectification}\,\xi^{C_{n}}(T)

\displaystyle=\textsf{evacuation}^{C_{n}}(\textsf{rectification}(T)).

(30)

Proof.

The crystal
\textsf{B}(T)\simeq\textsf{B}(\nu)
for some partition
\nu
and
\textsf{rectification}(T)\in\textsf{B}(\nu)
.
The full Schützenberger-Lusztig involution on KN tableaux of straight shape satisfies

\xi^{C_{n}}(\textsf{rect}(T))=\textsf{evacuation}^{C_{n}}(\textsf{rect}(T))
, and crystal
operators commute with SJDT when passing from
\textsf{B}(T)
to
\textsf{B}(\nu)
. Therefore, (
30
) holds.
∎

In Subsection
9.2.1
we will provide an algorithm for partial symplectic reversal on
KN

J

(λ,n)
with
J=[j,n]
. An algorithm for full
C_{n}
reversal on
\textsf{KN}(\lambda/\mu,n)
will result as a special case by considering the normal full sub-crystal
\textsf{B}(\mu,\lambda)
of
KN

J

(λ,n)
.

8.
Internal cactus group action on a normal crystal

8.1.
Partial Schützenberger-Lusztig involutions

Partial Schützenberger involutions were first studied in the case
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C})
by Berenstein and Kirillov

[
bk95
]
but have been defined by Halacheva in general for
g
: given
J\subseteq I
any sub-diagram, the partial Schützenberger–Lusztig involution
ξ

J

is defined to be the Schützenberger–Lusztig involution
\xi_{\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{Bj}{{{}}\textsf{B}_{J}}}
on the normal crystal
B

J

[
hakarywe20
]
.
The crystal
B

J

decomposes into connected components, and we apply the Schützenberger–Lusztig involution to each connected component. Let
b\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{B}{{{}}\textsf{B}}
, and let
u^{\textsf{high}}
,
u^{\textsf{low}}
be the highest and lowest weight elements of the connected component of
B

J

containing
b
. Let
b=f_{j_{r}}\cdots f_{j_{1}}(u^{\textsf{high}})
, with
j_{r}\cdots{j_{1}}\in J
. Then, for
j\in J
,

\displaystyle\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xij}{{{}}\xi_{\textsf{J}}}e_{j}(b)

\displaystyle=f_{\theta_{J}(j)}\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xij}{{{}}\xi_{\textsf{J}}}(b),

(31)

\displaystyle\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xij}{{{}}\xi_{\textsf{J}}}f_{j}(b)

\displaystyle=e_{\theta_{J}(j)}\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xij}{{{}}\xi_{\textsf{J}}}(b),

(32)

\displaystyle\textsf{wt}_{J}(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xij}{{{}}\xi_{\textsf{J}}}(b))

\displaystyle=w_{0}^{J}\textsf{wt}_{J}(b),

(33)

and

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xij}{{{}}\xi_{\textsf{J}}}(b)=e_{\theta_{J}(j_{r})}\cdots e_{\theta_{J}(j_{1})}(u^{\textsf{low}}).

Remark 11
.

If
J=K\cup K^{\prime}\subseteq I
is disconnected with
K
and
K^{\prime}
connected sub-diagrams of
I
, we have the sub-type Dynkin
diagram
K\times K^{\prime}
, and the Weyl group is
W^{K}\times W^{K^{\prime}}
with longest elements
w_{0}^{K}
and
w_{0}^{K^{\prime}}
, respectively, such that

w_{0}^{J}=w_{0}^{K}w_{0}^{K^{\prime}}=w_{0}^{K^{\prime}}w_{0}^{K}
. The weight lattice of
\mathfrak{g}_{K}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{K^{\prime}}
is
\Lambda_{K\cup K^{\prime}}:=\Lambda_{K}\oplus\Lambda_{K^{\prime}}
(see
[
BSch17
]
).
Then if
\theta_{K}
and
\theta_{K^{\prime}}
are the graph automorphisms defined by
w_{0}^{K}
and
w_{0}^{K^{\prime}}
in
K

and
K^{\prime}
, respectively,
\theta_{J}=\theta_{K}\theta_{K^{\prime}}=\theta_{K^{\prime}}\theta_{K}
is a graph automorphism of the Dynkin graph
K\times K^{\prime}
and hence
preserves the connected sub-diagrams
K
and
K^{\prime}
of
I
as defined in Section
3
.
Thanks to
[
ha16
, Lemmas 10.1.3, 10.1.4]
,
[
hakarywe20
, 2368–2369]
, the crystal operators act componentwise on the normal
crystal
\textsf{B}_{K\cup K^{\prime}}
(a normal
\mathfrak{g}_{K}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{K^{\prime}}
–crystal), and satisfy the following properties

\displaystyle f_{k}f_{k^{\prime}}=f_{k^{\prime}}f_{k},\,f_{k}e_{k^{\prime}}=e_{k^{\prime}}f_{k},\,e_{k}e_{k^{\prime}}=e_{k^{\prime}}e_{k},\,e_{k}f_{k^{\prime}}=f_{k^{\prime}}e_{k},\hbox{ for }k\in K,k^{\prime}\in K^{\prime},

(34)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{k}(e_{k^{\prime}}(b))=\varepsilon_{k}(b),\,\varphi_{k}(e_{k^{\prime}}(b))=\varphi_{k}(b),\,\hbox{ for }k\in K,k^{\prime}\in K^{\prime},\hbox{ and $e_{k^{\prime}}(b)\neq 0$
}

\displaystyle e_{k^{\prime}}\xi_{\textsf{B}_{K}}=\xi_{\textsf{B}_{K}}e_{k^{\prime}},\,f_{k^{\prime}}\xi_{\textsf{B}_{K}}=\xi_{\textsf{B}_{K}}f_{k^{\prime}},\hbox{ for }k\in K,k^{\prime}\in K^{\prime},

(35)

\displaystyle e_{k}\xi_{\textsf{B}_{K^{\prime}}}=\xi_{\textsf{B}_{K^{\prime}}}e_{k},\,f_{k}\xi_{\textsf{B}_{K^{\prime}}}=\xi_{\textsf{B}_{K^{\prime}}}f_{k},\hbox{ for }k\in K,k^{\prime}\in K^{\prime}.

(36)

This extends to a disconnected sub-diagram with more than two connected sub-diagrams. Henceforth, from
[
ha16
,
hakarywe20
]
,
\xi_{K}
and
\xi_{K^{\prime}}
commute

\xi_{K}\xi_{K^{\prime}}=\xi_{K^{\prime}}\xi_{K}.

Lemma 6
.

Let
J=K\cup K^{\prime}\subseteq I
be a disconnected sub-diagram of
I
with
K
and
K^{\prime}
connected. Then
\textsf{B}_{K\cup K^{\prime}}
is a normal crystal, and the Schützenberger–Lusztig involution on
\textsf{B}_{K\cup K^{\prime}}
,
\xi_{K\cup K^{\prime}}
satisfies

\xi_{K\cup K^{\prime}}=\xi_{K}\xi_{K^{\prime}}=\xi_{K^{\prime}}\xi_{K}.

Proof.

The result follows from the previous remark:
\xi_{K}\xi_{K^{\prime}}=\xi_{K^{\prime}}\xi_{K}
is an involution, and from
(
34
), (
35
) and (
36
),
it satisfies
the conditions (
31
), (
32
) above. In addition, the weight map
\textsf{wt}_{k\cup k^{\prime}}:\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{B}{{{}}\textsf{B}}\overset{\textsf{wt}}{\rightarrow}\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda_{K\cup K^{\prime}}=\Lambda_{K}\oplus\Lambda_{K^{\prime}}
and
therefore,
\textsf{wt}_{k\cup k^{\prime}}(\xi_{K}\xi_{K^{\prime}}(b))=(\textsf{wt}_{K}(\xi_{K}(b)),\textsf{wt}_{K^{\prime}}(\xi_{K^{\prime}}(b))=w_{0}^{K}w_{0}^{K^{\prime}}(\textsf{wt}_{K}(b),\textsf{wt}_{K^{\prime}}(b))
.
Since there is only one set involution on
\textsf{B}_{K\cup K^{\prime}}

satisfying (
31
), (
32
), and (
33
), we have that
\xi_{K\cup K^{\prime}}=\xi_{K}\xi_{K^{\prime}}=\xi_{K^{\prime}}\xi_{K}.

∎

The partial Schützenberger–Lusztig involutions
ξ

J

, for any
J\subseteq I
a
connected Dynkin sub-diagram
of
I
, satisfy the
J_{\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}}}
cactus relations.

Theorem 1

(
[
ha16
]
)
.

The map
s_{J}\mapsto\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xij}{{{}}\xi_{\textsf{J}}}
, for all
J\subseteq I
connected Dynkin sub-diagrams of
I
, defines an action of the cactus group
J_{\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}}}
on the set
B
; that is, the following is a group homomorphism

\begin{array}[]{cccccc}\Phi_{\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}}}:&J_{\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}}}&\rightarrow&\mathfrak{S}_{\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{B}{{{}}\textsf{B}}}\\
&s_{J}&\mapsto&\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xij}{{{}}\xi_{\textsf{J}}}.\\
\end{array}

Moreover

\textsf{wt}_{J}(\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xij}{{{}}\xi_{\textsf{J}}}(b))=w_{0}^{J}\textsf{wt}_{J}(b)
,
b\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{B}{{{}}\textsf{B}}
.

In other words,
s_{J}
acts on each connected component of
B
by permuting its vertices via
ξ

J

exchanging highest weight and lowest
weight elements.

Remark 12
.

(1)
The action of
J_{\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}}}
factorizes through the quotient by the braid relations of
W^{\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}}}
.

(2)
The partial Schützenberger–Lusztig involutions satisfy the cactus relations, and, in particular, for

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C})
, and
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{g}{{{}}\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C}),\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C})
, it holds that

\xi^{A_{n-1}}_{[n-i,n-1]}=\xi^{A_{n-1}}_{[1,n-1]}\xi^{A_{n-1}}_{[1,i]}\xi^{A_{n-1}}_{[1,n-1]},\,1\leq i<n,\;\;\xi^{C_{n}}_{[i,n]}=\xi^{C_{n}}_{[1,n]}\xi^{C_{n}}_{[i,n]}\xi^{C_{n}}_{[1,n]},\,1\leq i\leq n,

respectively.

The following corollary motivates what comes in the next section.

Corollary 3
.

(a)
For the
\mathfrak{sl}(n,\mathbb{C})
-crystal

\displaystyle s_{[1,j]}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\xi^{C_{n}}_{[1,j]},\;\quad 1\leq j\leq n-1,

(37)

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~s_{[j,n]}

defines an action of
J

s
p
(

,

9.1.
Dynkin sub-diagram with a sole node and the Weyl group action

Let

Theorem 4
.

[
kash94

\{\xi_{i}:i\in I\}

on the underlying set of

,
r_{i}.b=\xi_{i}(b)

The

\varphi_{i}(\xi_{i}(b))=\varepsilon_{i}(b)

\varepsilon_{i}(\xi_{i}(b))=\varphi_{i}(b)

\xi_{i}(b)

\scriptsize e_{i}^{\scriptsize\varepsilon_{i}(b)}(\scriptsize b)
\varepsilon_{i}(b)
\varphi_{i}(b)

Next propositions are a follow-up of the action of the Weyl group on
i
-strings where useful information is gathered.

Proposition 6
.

(1)

For

and the alphabet

n

: given

, let

, and let

. Then, for all

,

r_{i}.(u^{-}v^{+})=\xi_{i}(u^{-}v^{+})=\begin{cases}u_{1}^{-}e_{i}^{r-s}(u_{2}^{-})v^{+},\;r>s\\
u^{-}v^{+},\,r=s\\
u^{-}f_{i}^{s-r}(v_{1}^{+})v_{2}^{+},\;r<s\end{cases},

(44)

such that when

,
u^{-}=u_{1}^{-}u_{2}^{-}
, with
\ell(u_{2}^{-})=r-s
, and when
r<s
,
v=v_{1}^{+}v_{2}^{+}
with
\ell(v_{1}^{+})=s-r
.

When
i=n
,

r_{n}.{\overline{n}}^{r}{n}^{s}=\xi_{n}({\overline{n}}^{r}{n}^{s})={\overline{n}}^{s}{n}^{r}.

(45)

(2)

If
b\in\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{cncrystal}{{{}}\textsf{B}(\lambda)}
with
b=f_{j_{r}}\cdots f_{j_{1}}(u_{\lambda})
, and
r_{k}\cdots r_{i}r_{j}
is a reduced word for
w_{0}\in W
, then

\displaystyle\lx@glossaries@gls@link{main}{xi}{{{}}\xi}(b)

\displaystyle=e_{\theta(j_{r})}\cdots e_{\theta(j_{1})}(r_{k}\cdots r_{i}r_{j}.u_{\lambda}).

(3)

For
U_{q}(\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{C}))
: the crystal reflection operators
\xi_{i}
satisfy the relations of the
Weyl group
B_{n}
:

•

\xi_{i}^{2}=1
,
1\leq i\leq n
,

•

\xi_{i}\xi_{j}=\xi_{j}\xi_{i}
,
|i-j|>1
,
1\leq i,j\leq n
,

•

(\xi_{i}\xi_{i+1})^{3}=1
,
1\leq i\leq n-2
,

•

(\xi_{n-1}\xi_{n})^{4}=1.

Example 6
.

From (
44
), (
45
), the action of
\xi_{i}
on a KN tableau is given by the signature rule on its reading word
[
kn91
,
lec02
]
